Division/Office	Division of Hematologic Malignancies II/Office of Oncologic Diseases
Review Completion Date	December 22, 2022
Established Name	Mosunetuzumab
(Proposed) Trade Name	Lunsumio
Pharmacologic Class	CD20-directed CD3 T-cell engaging bispecific antibody
Applicant	Genentech, Inc.
Formulation(s)	Solution in a single-dose vial
Dosing Regimen	1 mg/1 mL, 30 mg/30 mL (1 mg/mL)
Applicant Proposed Indication(s)/Population(s)	Adult patients with relapsed or refractory follicular lymphoma after at least two prior systemic therapies
Recommendation on Regulatory Action	Accelerated Approval
Recommended Indication(s)/Population(s) (if applicable)	The treatment of adult patients with relapsed or refractory follicular lymphoma after two or more lines of systemic therapy

L44: ## 1 Executive Summary
L45: ## 1.1. Product Introduction
L46: Product: Mosunetuzumab (LUNSUMIO)
L47: Pharmacological Class: Bispecific CD20-directed CD3 T-cell engager.
L48: Proposed Indication: Mosunetuzumab is a bispecific CD20-directed CD3 T-cell engager indicated for the treatment of adult patients with relapsed or refractory follicular lymphoma after at least two prior systemic therapies.
L49: Dosing regimen: The recommended dosage of mosunetuzumab is 1 mg on Cycle 1, Day 1; 2 mg on Cycle 1 Day 8; 60 mg on Cycle 1 Day 15 and Cycle 2 Day 1; and 30 mg on Cycle 3 Day 1 onward, administered every 3 weeks.
L50: ## 1.2. Conclusions on the Substantial Evidence of Effectiveness
L51: The FDA review team recommends accelerated approval of mosunetuzumab for the following indication:
L52: *Mosunetuzumab is a bispecific CD20-directed CD3 T-cell engager indicated for the treatment of adult patients with relapsed or refractory follicular lymphoma after two or more lines of systemic therapy.*
L53: This recommendation is based on the efficacy of mosunetuzumab from GO29781, an open-label, multicenter, multicohort study in 90 patients with relapsed or refractory follicular lymphoma (FL) who had received at least two prior therapies, including an anti-CD20 monoclonal antibody and an alkylating agent.
L54: Patients received step-up doses of 1 mg on Cycle 1 Day 1 and 2 mg on Cycle 1 Day 8, followed by 60 mg on Cycle 1 Day 15, and 60 mg on Cycle 2 Day 1, then 30 mg every 3 weeks in subsequent cycles. A treatment cycle was 21 days. Mosunetuzumab was administered for 8 cycles unless patients experienced progressive disease or unacceptable toxicity. After 8 cycles, patients with a complete response discontinued therapy; patients with a partial response or stable disease continued treatment up to 17 cycles, unless patients experienced progressive disease or unacceptable toxicity.
L55: In the 90 patients with relapsed or refractory FL enrolled in the GO29781 study, the median age was 60 years (range: 29 to 90 years), 33% were 65 years of age or older, 61% were male, 82%
L56: were White, 9% were Asian, 4% were Black or African American, and 8% were Hispanic or Latino. A total of 77% of patients had Stage III-IV disease, 34% had bulky disease, and all patients had an Eastern Cooperative Oncology Group (ECOG) performance status of 0 or 1. The median number of prior therapies was 3 (range: 2 to 10), with 38% receiving 2 prior therapies, 31% receiving 3 prior therapies, and 31% receiving more than 3 prior therapies.
L57: Efficacy was established on the basis of objective response rate (ORR) and duration of response (DOR) as assessed by an independent review facility (IRF) according to standard criteria for NHL (Cheson 2007). Among the 90 patients, the ORR per IRF was 80% (95% CI: 70, 88), with 60% achieving a complete response. With a median follow-up for DOR of 14.9 months, the estimated median DOR was 22.8 months (95% CI: 10, not reached). Of the 72 responders, 62% remained in response at 12 months and 57% at 18 months.
L58: In this population, the overall response rate of 80%, with 60% achieving complete response, with durability and a tolerable safety profile constitutes a meaningful clinical benefit. To support accelerated approval, the efficacy data are considered in the context of available therapy for the patients with relapsed or refractory follicular lymphoma in the 3rd line setting and beyond. Available therapy includes anti-CD20 therapy (i.e., rituximab and obinutuzumab) as a single agent and in combination, bendamustine monotherapy and in combination, and immunotherapy combinations such as rituximab plus lenalidomide. In the 3rd line setting and beyond, the ORRs for these therapies, including single-agent and combinations, ranges from 57% to 80% with associated durability. In the 90 patients with FL treated with mosunetuzumab, all patients received prior anti-CD20 monoclonal antibody and an alkylating agent and the median number of prior therapies was 3. A total of 79% of patients were refractory to prior anti-CD20 monoclonal antibody therapy, 53% were refractory to both anti-CD20 monoclonal antibody and alkylator therapy, 9% received prior rituximab plus lenalidomide therapy, 21% received prior autologous stem cell transplant, and 3% received prior CAR T therapy. Given the prior therapies received, the overall response rate of 80% with associated durability for mosunetuzumab provides data to support a clinically meaningful benefit in the context of available therapy for patients with relapsed or refractory follicular lymphoma in the 3rd line setting and beyond.
L59: Thus, the efficacy data for mosunetuzumab demonstrates substantial evidence of effectiveness in support of accelerated approval for the following indication:
L60: - Treatment of adult patients with relapsed or refractory follicular lymphoma after two or more lines of systemic therapy
L61: ## 1.3. Benefit-Risk Assessment (BRA)
L62: ## Benefit-Risk Summary and Assessment
L63: The benefit-risk assessment is favorable for mosunetuzumab, a bispecific CD20-directed CD3 T-cell engager indicated for the treatment of adult patients with relapsed or refractory follicular lymphoma after two or more lines of systemic therapy.
L64: ## Efficacy:
L65: Efficacy of mosunetuzumab is based on the results from GO29781, an open-label, multicenter, multicohort study in 90 patients with relapsed or refractory follicular lymphoma who had received at least two prior therapies, including an anti-CD20 monoclonal antibody and an alkylating agent.
L66: Patients received step-up doses of 1 mg on Cycle 1 Day 1 and 2 mg on Cycle 1 Day 8, followed by 60 mg on Cycle 1 Day 15, and 60 mg on Cycle 2 Day 1, then 30 mg every 3 weeks in subsequent cycles. A treatment cycle was 21 days. Mosunetuzumab was administered for 8 cycles unless patients experienced progressive disease or unacceptable toxicity. After 8 cycles, patients with a complete response discontinued therapy; patients with a partial response or stable disease continued treatment up to 17 cycles, unless patients experienced progressive disease or unacceptable toxicity.
L67: Efficacy was established on the basis of objective response rate (ORR) and duration of response (DOR) as assessed by an independent review facility (IRF) according to standard criteria for NHL (Cheson 2007). Among the 90 patients, the ORR per IRF was 80% (95% CI: 70, 88), with 60% achieving a complete response. With a median follow-up for DOR of 14.9 months, the estimated median DOR was 22.8 months (95% CI: 10, not reached). Of the 72 responders, 62% remained in response at 12 months and 57% at 18 months.
L68: ## Safety:
L69: The safety profile of mosunetuzumab was primarily supported by analysis of 218 patients with B-cell NHL that received the recommended dose and regimen (1/2/60/30 mg). The median duration of treatment was 8 cycles (range 1 to 17), with 52% exposed for at least 8 cycles. The most common adverse events (\ge 20\%) were cytokine release syndrome (39%), fatigue (36%), rash (34%), pyrexia (24%), and headache (21%). The most common Grade 3 to 4 laboratory abnormalities (\ge 10\%) were decreased lymphocyte count (92%), decreased phosphate (41%), increased glucose (40%), decreased neutrophil count (38%), increased uric acid (15%), decreased white blood cell count (22%), decreased hemoglobin
L70: (19%), and decreased platelets (12%). Serious adverse events occurred in 52%, most often due to CRS (21%), pneumonia (5%), pyrexia (5%), sepsis (3%), and a 2% incidence each of tumor flare, urinary tract infection, pleural effusion, and renal insufficiency. Adverse events leading to treatment interruption occurred in 33%, most often due to neutropenia, infection, and CRS. Adverse events leading to treatment discontinuation occurred in 4%, most often due to CRS. Thus, the primary safety issues identified with mosunetuzumab include CRS, neurologic toxicity (including immune effector cell-associated neurotoxicity syndrome [ICANS]), infections, cytopenias, and tumor flare.
L71: ## Benefit-Risk:
L72: Mosunetuzumab has an overall favorable benefit-risk in patients with relapsed or refractory follicular lymphoma after two or more lines of systemic therapy. In the 90 patients with relapsed or refractory FL enrolled in the GO29781 study, the median age was 60 years (range: 29 to 90 years), 33% were 65 years of age or older, 61% were male, 82% were White, 9% were Asian, 4% were Black or African American, and 8% were Hispanic or Latino. A total of 77% of patients had Stage III-IV disease, 34% had bulky disease, and all patients had an Eastern Cooperative Oncology Group (ECOG) performance status of 0 or 1. The median number of prior therapies was 3 (range: 2 to 10), with 38% receiving 2 prior therapies, 31% receiving 3 prior therapies, and 31% receiving more than 3 prior therapies.
L73: In this population, the overall response rate of 80%, with 60% achieving complete response, with durability and a tolerable safety profile constitutes a meaningful clinical benefit and a favorable benefit-risk evaluation. To support accelerated approval, the efficacy data are considered in the context of available therapy for the patients with relapsed or refractory follicular lymphoma in the 3rd line setting and beyond. Available therapy includes anti-CD20 therapy (i.e., rituximab and obinutuzumab) as a single agent and in combination, bendamustine monotherapy and in combination, and immunotherapy combinations such as rituximab plus lenalidomide. In the 3rd line setting and beyond, the ORRs for these therapies, including single-agent and combinations, ranges from 57% to 80% with associated durability. In the 90 patients with FL, the median number of prior therapies was 3 and included an anti-CD20 monoclonal antibody and an alkylating agent. A total of 79% of patients were refractory to prior anti-CD20 monoclonal antibody therapy, 53% were refractory to both anti-CD20 monoclonal antibody and alkylator therapy, 9% received prior rituximab plus lenalidomide therapy, 21% received prior autologous stem cell transplant, and 3% received prior CAR T therapy. Therefore, the overall response of 80% with associated durability for mosunetuzumab provides data to support a clinically meaningful benefit in the context of available therapy for patients with relapsed or refractory follicular lymphoma in the 3rd line setting and beyond.
L74: Thus, the benefit-risk of mosunetuzumab is deemed favorable to support accelerated approval for the following indication:
L75: - Treatment of adult patients with relapsed or refractory follicular lymphoma after two or more lines of systemic therapy

Analysis of Condition	Follicular lymphoma (FL) is the second most common non-Hodgkin lymphoma.
Follicular lymphoma remains an incurable disease and relapse is nearly universal.
Follicular lymphoma is a serious and life-threatening disease.
Current Treatment Options	Treatment options for relapsed and refractory follicular lymphoma include anti-CD20 monoclonal antibodies, immunochemotherapy, an immunomodulatory agent, a PI3K inhibitor, an EZH2 inhibitor, and anti-CD19 CAR-T cell therapy.
New treatments are needed for patients with relapsed or refractory follicular lymphoma.

Mosunetuzumab was administered as follows: 1 mg on Cycle 1 Day 1; 2 mg on Cycle 1 Day 8; 60 mg on Cycle 1 Day 15 and Cycle 2 Day 1; and 30 mg on Cycle 3 Day 1 onwards, administered every 3 weeks.

With a median follow-up for duration of response (DOR) of 14.9 months, the median DOR was 22.8 months (95% CI: 10, not reached).
Based on ORR with durability, mosunetuzumab has clinically meaningful activity in patients with relapsed or refractory follicular lymphoma who have received two or more lines of systemic therapy.

The median duration of treatment was 8 cycles.
The most common adverse events (\ge 20\%) were cytokine release syndrome, fatigue, rash, pyrexia, and headache.
The safety profile of mosunetuzumab is acceptable in the intended population.

L86: ## 2 Therapeutic Context
L87: ## 2.1. Analysis of Condition
L88: ## The Applicant's Position:
L89: Follicular lymphoma (FL) is the second most common non-Hodgkin lymphoma (NHL), accounting for an estimated 13,960 new cases diagnosed in the US in 2016 (Teras et al. 2016). In the US, FL is more frequently diagnosed in White (~92%) than in Black (~4%) or Asian/Pacific Islander (~4%) racial groups (Li et al. 2015; Blansky et al. 2020), while the incidence rates in men and women are similar. The incidence increases with age, and the median age of diagnosis of FL in the US is 63 years (SEER Cancer Statistics 2020).
L90: FL tumor cells are malignant counterparts of normal germinal center B cells. In 85% of cases, cytogenetic analysis reveals the genetic hallmark of t(14;18)(q32;q21) genomic translocation, which results in the constitutive overexpression of the BCL-2 protein, a member of a family of proteins that regulates apoptosis. Almost all cases of FL carry additional genetic alterations (Hiddemann and Cheson 2014). The FL immunophenotype usually shows CD10+, CD20+, CD19+, CD22+ CD5- cells expressing surface immunoglobulin.
L91: The high degree of mutational heterogeneity in FL is reflected in a highly variable clinical behavior, ranging from an extremely indolent course over decades to a clinically highly aggressive course with short DOR after treatment and recurrence of disease within 1-2 years after initial treatment (Freedman and Jacobsen 2020). Histologic transformation of FL from an indolent disease to the more aggressive DLBCL occurs with a risk of 2-3% per year, and is associated with more aggressive disease progression and poor survival outcome (Montoto and Fitzgibbon 2011; Link et al. 2013). Typically, the clinical course of FL is characterized by a series of remissions and relapses, with generally increasing refractoriness and decreasing duration of response to therapy. As the disease recurs, patients are treated with multiple lines of therapies during their lifetime. FL remains incurable, and a significant proportion of patients die from recurrent disease or treatment-related toxicity.
L92: Patients with FL typically present with asymptomatic lymphadenopathy, however the majority of patients are diagnosed with advanced disease (Ann Arbor Stage III/IV), with up to 70% with bone marrow involvement (Freedman and Jacobsen 2020). The Follicular Lymphoma International Prognostic Index (FLIPI) is the most widely used prognostic scoring system to predict survival in newly diagnosed patients, though it may be prognostic at first relapse as well (Montoto et al. 2004).
L93: Observation (watch-and-wait) is the standard practice for patients with advanced stage low
L94: tumor burden FL. Guidelines recommend that treatment is only initiated upon occurrence of one or more of the modified GELF criteria (Brice et al. 1997; NCCN Guidelines v4.2021). For patients with FL for whom initiating systemic treatment is indicated, immunochemotherapy with an anti-CD20 monoclonal antibody (rituximab or obinutuzumab) and chemotherapy (bendamustine, CHOP or CVP), with or without extended maintenance dosing with rituximab or obinutuzumab monotherapy for up to 2 years is the standard of care for first-line treatment.
L95: After initiating treatment, quality of response (e.g., achieving a CR, PCR-negative MRD in blood or bone marrow, or negative PET at end of treatment) is associated with improved OS (Bachy et al. 2010; Trotman et al 2018). A significantly higher risk of death is observed in patients with early progression of disease, specifically within 24 months of commencing first-line immunochemotherapy (Casulo et al. 2017; Seymour et al. 2019). Beyond the front-line setting, prognosis is influenced by several factors, including number of prior regimens, refractory status, and progressive decline of bone marrow reserve (Smith 2013). In patients with FL who progress from front-line therapies, the disease-free intervals and DOR become progressively shorter with increased refractoriness with each subsequent progression/relapse (Link et al. 2019; Rivas-Delgado et al. 2019). Patients who have received \ge 2 prior therapies have a particularly poor prognosis, with a median PFS ranging from 1-1.1 years for third-line patients decreasing to 0.5 years for sixth-line patients with a corresponding median OS of 4.8-8.8 years and 1.9 years, respectively (Alperovich et al. 2016; Rivas-Delgado et al. 2019; Batlevi et al. 2020).
L96: ## The FDA's Assessment:
L97: The FDA agrees with the Applicant's position. Using data from the US Surveillance, Epidemiology, and End Results (SEER program), the age adjusted incidence rate based on 2015-2019 cases was 2.6 per 100000 men and women. Rates were higher in men (2.8) among all races than among women (2.4). Among men, rates were highest in Non-Hispanic Whites (3.2), followed by Hispanics (2.6), Non-Hispanic American/Indian Alaskan Natives (1.9), Non-Hispanic Asian/Pacific Islanders (1.7), and Non-Hispanic Blacks (1.3). Among women, rates were also highest among Non-Hispanic Whites (2.7), followed by Hispanics (2.4), Non-Hispanic American/Indian Alaskan Natives (1.8), Non-Hispanic Asian/Pacific Islanders (1.4), and Non-Hispanic Blacks (1.2).
L98: ## 2.2. Analysis of Current Treatment Options
L99: ## The Applicant's Position:
L100: Relapse to initial therapy for FL is almost inevitable. In the relapsed/refractory setting, treatment decisions take into consideration efficacy and DOR of prior therapy, stage of disease and tumor burden at relapse, the presence of symptoms, and the age and comorbidities of the patient.
L101: For patients who require second-line therapy, there is no treatment considered standard of
L102: care, and options vary widely. Rituximab as a single agent, bendamustine, obinutuzumab in combination with bendamustine followed by obinutuzumab monotherapy, lenalidomide plus a rituximab-product, and the radioimmunotherapy 90Y-ibritumomab tiuxetan have all received regular full approval for the treatment of R/R FL in the US.
L103: In the third-line setting, certain treatment options approved for first- and/or second-line FL are available treatment options. However, patients who have become refractory to anti-CD20-directed therapy or alkylating agents will unlikely derive treatment benefit by re-using these regimens. In later relapses, a non-chemotherapy approach is recommended. Agents under accelerated approval specifically for patients with relapsed or refractory FL after at least two prior therapies (and not considered to be available therapy because clinical benefit is yet to be confirmed) are the PI3K inhibitors idelalisib, copanlisib, and duvelisib, and the histone methyltransferase enhancer of zeste homolog 2 (EZH2) inhibitor tazemetostat, and the CD19-directed chimeric antigen receptor-modified T-cell (CAR-T) therapy axicabtagene ciloleucel (Yescarta®). Umbralisib, a dual PI3K delta and casein kinase 1 epsilon inhibitor, has received accelerated approval for the treatment of patients with R/R FL who have received at least three prior lines of systemic therapy.
L104: Specific details of the indications, and the efficacy and safety data supporting FDA-approved drugs for patients with indications relevant to this FL application are listed in Table 1 below. Not included in the table are chemotherapeutic agents (e.g., chlorambucil, cyclophosphamide, vincristine, and doxorubicin) which are approved for use in the US to treat a broader spectrum of neoplastic diseases including malignant lymphomas.
L105: In addition to approved therapies, there are chemotherapy agents or combinations that are used in the R/R setting, including some that are not FDA approved. Table 2 summarizes suggested treatment regimens for second-line and subsequent therapy, and third-line and subsequent therapy for the treatment of FL as described in the current National Comprehensive Cancer Network® Clinical Practice Guidelines in Oncology for B-cell Lymphomas (v4.2021).
L106: Relapsed or refractory FL is a serious and life-threatening disease that remains incurable. Relapse is almost inevitable, and a significant proportion of patients die from recurrent disease or treatment-related toxicity. There is a high unmet need for patients with R/R FL who have received ≥2 prior therapies, particularly for patients who are R/R to different classes of agents and are left with limited treatment options that may have challenging safety profiles. New treatment options that will significantly extend duration of remission and that can overcome resistance to existing therapies, while providing acceptable safety and tolerability are needed.

L120: ## The FDA's Assessment:
L121: In general, the FDA agrees with the Applicant's position, but has provided additional information. Per the Federal Register, the indications in follicular lymphoma for duvelisib, idealisib, and umbralisib were voluntarily withdrawn on 4/13/22, 5/26/22, and 5/31/22, respectively. Copanlisib remains under accelerated approval for the treatment of adult patients with relapsed follicular lymphoma who have received at least 2 prior systemic therapies. Tisagenlecleucel, a C19-directed genetically modified autologous T cell immunotherapy, also recently received accelerated approval on May 31, 2022, for adult patients with relapsed or refractory follicular lymphoma after two or more lines of systemic therapy.

L139: ## 4 Significant Issues from Other Review Disciplines Pertinent to Clinical Conclusions on Efficacy and Safety
L140: ## 4.1. Office of Scientific Investigations (OSI)
L141: Clinical site inspections were requested for two clinical sites in the U.S. with the highest accrual for Study GO29781, along with inspection of the Applicant, Genentech, Inc. The clinical sites included 1) Site # 284510 – Washington University School of Medicine (Dr. Bartlett, MD; St. Louis, MO), which enrolled 40 patients and 2) Site 284773 – City of Hope (Dr. Budde, MD; Duarte, CA), which enrolled 66 patients. For both clinical sites inspections, no objectionable conditions or practices were identified, and no actions were indicated. Therefore, based on the clinical site inspections, the study data from the clinical sites were considered reliable. Additionally, the Applicant was inspected concerning its oversight of GO29781. Based on the inspection, no objectionable conditions or practices were identified, and no actions were indicated. Thus, the Applicant's oversight of the clinical trial and the quality of conducting the study were determined to be adequate. The audited study data submitted to the Agency was deemed acceptable in support of the BLA application, in compliance with Good Clinical Practice.
L142: ## 4.2. Product Quality
L143: Mosunetuzumab is bispecific CD20-directed CD3 T-cell engager. It is a humanized monoclonal anti-CD20xCD3 T-cell-dependent bispecific antibody of the immunoglobulin G1 (IgG1) isotype. Mosunetuzumab-axgb is produced in Chinese Hamster Ovary (CHO) cells by recombinant DNA technology. The approximate molecular weight is 146 kDa.
L144: Mosunetuzumab-axgb injection is a sterile, preservative-free, colorless solution for intravenous use.
L145: Each single-dose vial contains a 1 mL solution of mosunetuzumab-axgb (1 mg), acetic acid (0.4 mg), histidine (1.6 mg), methionine (1.5 mg), polysorbate 20 (0.6 mg), sucrose (82.1 mg), and Water for Injection, USP. The pH is 5.8.
L146: Each single-dose vial contains a 30 mL solution of mosunetuzumab-axgb (30 mg), acetic acid (12.8 mg), histidine (46.6 mg), methionine (44.8 mg), polysorbate 20 (18 mg), sucrose (2,462.4 mg), and Water for Injection, USP. The pH is 5.8.
L147: The Office of Pharmaceutical Quality recommends approval of mosunetuzumab-axgb manufactured by Genentech, Inc. for human use under the conditions specified in the package insert. The data submitted support the conclusion that the manufacture of mosunetuzumab-axgb is well-controlled and leads to a product that is pure and potent.

L152: ## 5 Nonclinical Pharmacology/Toxicology
L153: ## 5.1. Executive Summary
L154: Mosunetuzumab (RO7030816; also known as BTCT4465A) is a full-length, humanized immunoglobulin (Ig) G1 bispecific antibody, produced in Chinese hamster ovary (CHO) cells, and directed against CD20 and CD3.
L155: The CD20 antigen is present on both healthy and malignant B-cells, and the CD3 receptor is expressed on the surface of T-cells. Mosunetuzumab promotes the T-cell dependent killing of B-cells through CD20 and therefore, is being developed for the treatment of patients with B-cell hematologic malignancies.
L156: The proposed mechanism of action of mosunetuzumab was demonstrated with a variety of in vitro and in vivo pharmacology studies. A version of the antibody manufactured in was used in early nonclinical studies prior to the development of the CHO-manufactured clinical product. A bridging PK/PD study in transgenic mice showed that the exposure profile and effect on B-cell levels were comparable between the 2 versions of the antibodies. In vitro activity studies showed that binding to CD3 and CD20 (CD3 Kd =40 nM; CD20 Kd=68-83 nM), T-cell activation (EC50 = 128-149 ng/mL), B-cell killing (EC50 = 36-60 ng/mL) and cytokine induction (IL-2, IL-6, IFN\gamma, and TNF\alpha) were also comparable between the antibodies. The cynomolgus monkey is a relevant species for toxicology studies based on mosunetuzumab binding to CD20 and CD3 in cynomolgus monkeys.
L157: The Applicant conducted multiple single and repeat dose pilot toxicity studies to find a dose and dosing regimen that would minimize ADA formation and cytokine-release syndrome (CRS)-like toxicities. One such design was the step-up dosing approach which was used in the repeated-dose study. The GLP studies in cynomolgus monkeys included single-dose and repeated-dose 26-week toxicology studies, both via the intravenous route. In the single-dose study, mosunetuzumab was administered at 0.01, 0.1, or 1.0 mg/kg. Inflammatory responses were noted in animals (e.g., increased C-reactive protein (CRP) and fibrinogen, decreased
L158: albumin, increased WBC differentials, and multi-organ infiltration) and are anticipated based on the mechanism of action that includes immune activation and cytokine release. Reductions in white blood cell counts and related pharmacodynamic effects on associated subpopulations were noted. Major toxicity findings included the following: clinical signs of emesis and decreased activity, transient increases in heart rate and body temperature with decreased blood pressure, increased CRP and bilirubin, decreased cholesterol and albumin levels, and microscopic findings. Microscopic findings were in the lymphoid system (decreased cellularity in the lymph nodes) and the central nervous system (CNS) that resolved, as well as inflammatory cell infiltrates in the vasculature, heart, liver, kidney, gastrointestinal tract, gall bladder, and lymphoid tissues. In the 26-week repeated-dose study, the animals were given 2 priming doses on dosing days 1 and 2 prior to weekly doses of 0.1 or 0.5 mg/kg for the rest of the dosing period. Two animals (one at 0.1 mg/kg and one at 0.5 mg/kg) were euthanized prematurely in moribund conditions due to immune suppression-mediated infection. Clinical signs included swelling, increased temperature, reduced movement, and emesis, which may be associated with mosunetuzumab-related cytokine release. Other findings in animals with unscheduled sacrifice were comparable to those in surviving animals and included the following: Reduced B-cells in the blood and lymphoid tissues, inflammatory signals such as increased monocyte, lymphocyte, eosinophil, and basophil counts in addition to increased globulin, CRP, and fibrinogen concentrations, and microscopic findings of inflammatory cell infiltration in multiple organs.
L159: While no animal studies were conducted to assess the reproductive and developmental risks of mosunetuzumab, a weight of evidence (WoE)-based risk assessment report was submitted. The assessment is based on a variety of factors, including maternal and/or neonatal toxicities due to depletion of B-cells and cytokine release-related events. Based on the drug's mechanism of action and pharmacodynamic effects, mosunetuzumab may cause depletion of B-cells and impair infant immune response due to fetal exposure, increasing the risk of opportunistic infections. Risks associated with T-cell activation include cytokine release and associated inflammatory effects, which may adversely affect a pregnant woman, and maintenance of pregnancy. Adverse events that may occur in pregnant animals are those that have been described in general toxicology studies, e.g., diarrhea, cardiac events, acute phase protein reactions and inflammatory response, B-cell depletion, etc. The recommendation for using contraception in females of reproductive potential for 3 months after the last dose of the product, as reflected in the product label, is based on 5 half-lives of the product.
L160: The nonclinical data of mosunetuzumab submitted to BLA 761263 are adequate to support the labeling and approval of mosunetuzumab for the proposed indication.

L170: To help bridge the studies and confirm the comparable pharmacological activities for mosunetuzumab (BTCT4465A CHO) (b) (4) in vitro assays (Study 14-3525) were conducted with human peripheral blood mononuclear cells (PBMCs) isolated from healthy donors to compare the binding affinity of the two drug products to the targets, and their potency in B-cell killing, T-cell activation, and cytokine production. The binding affinities of mosunetuzumab (BTCT4465A CHO) (b) (4) to human CD20 and CD3 (Study 14-3525) were comparable as measured using a scatchard assay in human BJAB and Jurkat cells. Both products demonstrated similar potency in activating CD8+ T-cells and killing endogenous B-cells in vitro, using human PBMCs from three independent donors (Table 4). The levels of cytokine production (IL-2, IL-6, IFN-\gamma, and TNF-\alpha) were comparable with (b) (4) (b) (4) CHO-derived BTCT4465A at pg/mL concentrations (Figure 1).

L178: The binding of the mosunetuzumab v0.1-derived drug product (used for the investigational new drug (IND)-enabling pharmacology, pharmacokinetic, and toxicology studies, as well as for the ongoing Phase 1 study) and mosunetuzumab v0.2-derived drug product (an updated v0.2 drug substance manufacturing process) to CD3\epsilon on T-cells and CD20 on B-cells in human and cynomolgus monkey PBMCs was assessed by flow cytometry (Study 17-2037). The two mosunetuzumab drug products demonstrated comparable binding to both CD20 and CD3\epsilon on B-cells and T-cells in the human and cynomolgus monkey (Figure 2). Binding studies with (b) (4) and mosunetuzumab confirm the cynomolgus monkey as an appropriate non-human primate model for the nonclinical toxicology studies for BTCT4465A.

L181: The following **in vitro** assays were performed to assess dependence of T-cell activation, and CD20+ target cell killing on Fc binding with (b) (4) (used in several POC studies), which binds to human CD3, but does not bind to cynomolgus monkey CD3 (Study 15-0391).
L182: **In vitro** cell killing was assessed against the target B-cell line BJAB (a CD20-expressing cell) using normal human donor PBMCs as effector cells in the presence of (b) (4) (intact TDB; lacking the Fc region). The potencies of cell killing of the F(ab')_2 fragment and intact TDB (T-cell-dependent bispecific) antibody were similar, suggesting anti-CD20/CD3 TDB-induced B-cell killing is not dependent on the Fc region (Figure 4A).
L183: **In vitro** T-cell-dependent B-cell killing was demonstrated by incubating PBMCs isolated from healthy donor or PBMCs depleted of CD3+ T-cells with various concentrations of (b) (4) for 24 hours. (b) (4) treatment resulted in concentration-
L184: dependent killing when incubated with CD3+ T-cells. However, depletion of CD3-expressing cells from PBMCs abolished the activity of (b) (4) indicating that T-cells are required for activity (Figure 4B).
L185: T-cell activation by the (b) (4) antibody was dependent on the presence of CD20+ target cells. BJAB cells and purified CD8+ T-cells or CD4+ T-cells were incubated with various concentrations of (b) (4) for 24 hours. The results show that T-cell activation marked as CD69+CD25+ was observed only in the presence of CD20+ target cells (Figure 4C).
L186: BJAB cells and purified CD8+ T-cells or CD4+ T-cells were incubated with various concentrations of (b) (4) for 24 hours. Consistent with CD20 cell killing, (b) (4) treatment resulted in greater T-cell induction and higher levels of perforin release were detected with activated CD8+ T-cells than with CD4+ T-cells (Figure 4D).

L192: Comparative in vivo pharmacokinetic and pharmacodynamic activity of mosunetuzumab or was evaluated in human CD20/CD3\epsilon double-transgenic B6N mice, in which
L193: human CD20 and CD3\epsilon are expressed on mouse B- and T-cells, respectively (Study 13-2919). Mice (n=12 per group) were given a single intravenous (IV) dose of 0.5 mg/kg BTCT4465A produced in either (b) (4) (Group 1) or CHO cells (Group 2). Serum samples were collected at 2, 8, and 24 hours after dosing and on Days 2 and 7, and assayed for BTCT4465A serum concentrations. Subsets of peripheral blood lymphocytes (helper T-cells [Th], cytotoxic T-cells [Tc], and B-cells) were collected and evaluated by flow cytometry for pharmacodynamic endpoints. Activation status of Th and Tc was evaluated by the early activation marker CD69, and B-cells were identified as CD45+ CD90.2+ CD19+ cells. In addition, at selected timepoints, spleen and lymph nodes were collected to evaluate pharmacodynamics by flow cytometry.

L206: ## Safety Pharmacology
L207: ## The Applicant's Position:
L208: Safety pharmacology evaluation was incorporated into single- and repeat-dose toxicity studies. Cardiovascular safety pharmacology findings included dose-dependent and reversible hypotension at 1 mg/kg IV, and tachycardia and increased core body temperature at
L209: \ge 0.1 mg/kg IV. With the exception of decreased RR, PR, and QT intervals due to increased heart rate, no quantitative or qualitative changes in electrocardiography were present. These findings are consistent with cytokine release and associated acute protein reactions. CNS findings were limited to one incidence of convulsion in one animal at high exposure multiples (refer to Section 5.5.1). There were no drug-related effects on respiratory endpoints.
L210: ## **The FDA's Assessment:**
L211: The FDA's assessment of safety pharmacology was made during the review of the GLP single-dose and repeat-dose studies in monkeys and is summarized in Section 5.5.1.
L212: ## 5.4. ADME/PK
L213: ## **The Applicant's Position:**
L214: In huCD20/CD3 transgenic mice, after a single IV dose, mosunetuzumab and [REDACTED](b) (4), a functionally equivalent antibody used in early nonclinical studies, demonstrated similar PK profiles and comparable B-cell killing in blood and tissues. In cynomolgus monkeys, after a single IV dose, mosunetuzumab exhibited dose-proportional exposure and rapid clearance (CL) at dose levels between 0.01 and 1 mg/kg. The dose proportionality at these doses was likely due to a large component of target-mediated CL. Following repeat IV dosing in cynomolgus monkeys, [REDACTED](b) (4) showed dose-dependent PK with higher exposure in later cycles, likely due to B-cell depletion and T cells returning to baseline after initial activation and expansion, leading to an eventual reduction of target-mediated CL.
L215: ## **The FDA's Assessment:**
L216: The FDA generally concurs with the Applicant's position.
L217: The Applicant conducted multiple single and repeat dose pilot studies to find a dose and dosing regimen that would minimize ADA formation and cytokine-release syndrome (CRS)-like toxicities.

L223: ## 5.5. Toxicology
L224: ## 5.5.1. General Toxicology
L225: ## The Applicant's Position:
L226: Mosunetuzumab has been evaluated in single-dose and repeat-dose toxicology studies up to 26 weeks in cynomolgus monkeys following IV administration. Key mosunetuzumab-related toxicities included post-dose CRS-like toxicities that were primarily limited to the first dose, reversible findings of vascular/perivascular inflammatory cell infiltrates, and increased susceptibility to infection following chronic dosing due to sustained B-cell depletion. All of the findings were considered pharmacologically-mediated effects.
L227: The most consistent findings were CRS-like toxicities following IV administration. They were primarily limited to the first dose and included tachycardia, fever, and acute phase reactions at \ge 0.1 mg/kg, clinical signs (e.g., emesis, hypoactivity) at \ge 0.2 mg/kg, and hypotension and evidence of minimal hepatocellular cell injury at 1 mg/kg. Microscopic findings of vascular/perivascular inflammatory cell infiltrates were present primarily within the brain, with decreased incidence in spinal cord and sciatic nerve in single and repeat-dose studies, and were also present infrequently in other organs in the 26-week toxicity study. In the brain, the findings were accompanied with local microglial reaction; however, no neuronal degeneration was observed. These findings were reversible and were considered secondary to cytokine/chemokine-induced up-regulation of chemokine receptors and adhesion molecules in the vessels of the brain and immune cell activation, margination, and transmigration. In-life neurologic abnormalities were limited to one incidence of convulsion in one animal on Day 8 following a total of 2 mg/kg dose, corresponding to Cmax and AUCDay0-7 values approximately 50-fold and 20-fold, respectively, higher than those in patients exposed to mosunetuzumab during the first two weeks of dosing (1 mg on Day 1 and 2 mg on Day 8) in Study GO29781. The
L228: CNS microscopic lesions in this animal were more extensive than those identified in the remaining animals and were considered causal for the convulsion. Following chronic dosing, mosunetuzumab was associated with microscopic evidence of upper urinary tract and/or enteric infection, which resulted in moribundity and early euthanasia in two animals. The increased susceptibility to infection was considered to be secondary to prolonged B-cell depletion, a pharmacologic effect of mosunetuzumab. With the exception of the above findings, no other toxicities were observed following 26 weeks of mosunetuzumab administration at the highest dose tested, corresponding to approximately 5-fold multiples relative to the steady-state exposure (AUC) in human at the recommended clinical dose of 1/2/60/30 mg.
L229: ## The FDA's Assessment:
L230: The GLP-compliant single-dose toxicity (IV/SC) and 26-week repeat-dose toxicity (IV) studies in cynomolgus monkeys were reviewed and we generally agree with the adverse findings described by the Applicant. Detailed reviews of the 26-week study and the summary of the single-dose toxicity study are included.
L231: ## **Study title/ Study number: BTCT4465A: 26 Week Multiple Dose Intravenous (Infusion) Administration Toxicity and Toxicokinetic Study in the Cynomolgus Monkey**
L232: ## **Key Study Findings**
L233: - Two animals (one at 0.1 mg/kg and one at 0.5 mg/kg) were euthanized prematurely in moribund conditions due to immune suppression/enteric infection.
- Clinical signs including red/swollen face, swollen eyes, warm to touch, subdued, and emesis occurred mostly during the step dosing phase, consistent with BTCT4465A-related cytokine release.
- Consistent with BTCT4465A pharmacological activity, markedly reduced B lymphocytes in the blood and lymphoid tissues, transient cytokine release and T-cell activation, and associated transient decrease in circulating T-cells and natural killer cells were observed at both dose levels tested.
- Clinical pathology showed increased indicators of inflammation (monocyte, lymphocyte, eosinophil, and basophil counts and globulin, CRP and fibrinogen concentrations).
- BTCT4465A-induced toxicity was observed within the systemic vasculature (vascular/perivascular inflammatory cell infiltration) in multiple tissues (brain, heart, liver, kidney, gastrointestinal tract, gall bladder), lymphoid tissues (decreased germinal centers/follicles/GALT), bone marrow (increased cellularity consistent with inflammation), kidney (inflammatory infiltration/tubular basophilia, likely related to ascending urinary tract infection), and pancreas (decreased exocrine granulation) in animals administered 0.1 or 0.5 mg/kg.
L234: GLP compliance: Yes

Toxicokinetics	The toxicokinetics information was presented and discussed in the pharmacokinetics section.
Anti-Drug Antibodies	ADAs were detected in 5 out of 8 Group 2 (0.1 mg/kg) animals and in 4 out of 8 Group 3 (0.5 mg/kg) animals administered BTCT4465A. L249: ## General toxicology; additional studies
L250: ## **Study title/number:** A Single Dose Toxicokinetic, Pharmacodynamic, and Toxicology Study of BTCT4465A via Intravenous Infusion or Subcutaneous Administration in Cynomolgus Monkeys with a 7-week Recovery Period/14-1246
L251: A GLP-compliant single-dose toxicology study of BTCT4465A in monkeys was conducted with IV infusion or subcutaneous (SC) injection once followed by a 7-week recovery period. Monkeys were dosed with BTCT4465A at 0 (IV/SC), 0.01 (IV), 0.1(IV), or 1 mg/kg (IV/SC). Necropsy procedures were performed on Day 8 (terminal; 3M/3F), Day 22 (first recovery necropsy; 2 M/2F per dose except 1M/2F at 1 mg/kg IV) and Day 57 (second recovery necropsy; 2M/2F each control and HD). The Day 22 necropsy was performed on animals instrumented with a telemetry device for collection of cardiovascular and body temperature data. Clinical signs included emesis, decreased activity/hunched posture and mucoid feces on Day 1 post-dose at 1 mg/kg IV.
L252: The cardiovascular and body temperature safety assessment on telemetered animals revealed transient and dose-dependent increases in heart rate and body temperature at 0.1 mg/kg IV and 1 mg/kg (IV or SC) and decreased blood pressure at 1 mg/kg IV on Day 1. Heart rate increases (RR interval decreases) at these dose levels had correlating decreases in PR and QT intervals (1 mg/kg IV and SC, only), but there was no effect on QTca. Cardiovascular findings resolved by Day 7.
L253: Changes in hematology parameters at \ge 0.01 mg/kg included decreased white blood cells associated with decreased lymphocytes and decreased monocytes and basophils. Changes in coagulation parameters included increased fibrinogen at \ge 0.1 mg/kg and prolongation of the
L254: prothrombin time and activated partial thromboplastin time at 1 mg/kg. Changes in clinical chemistry parameters, included increased CRP at \ge 0.01 mg/kg, increased bilirubin and decreased cholesterol and albumin (with associated decreases in total protein, albumin to globulin ratio and calcium) at \ge 0.1 mg/kg, and increased urea nitrogen and creatinine, and decreased sodium and chloride at 1 mg/kg. Clinical pathology changes tended to recover by Day 8 through Day 22 or Day 57.
L255: BTCT4465A-related microscopic findings were observed in tissues including the lymphoid system (spleen, thymus, lymph nodes and/or gut associated lymphoid tissue [GALT]), liver, heart, and central nervous system (CNS) at \ge 0.01 mg/kg IV and 1 mg/kg SC. In the spleen, increased lymphocyte cellularity within the periarteriolar sheaths (PALS) and increased phagocytic cells within the red pulp correlated with increased spleen weights. In the thymus, decreased lymphocyte cellularity was noted that correlated with decreased thymus weights. In GALT, decreased number and size of follicular germinal centers, and decreased number and size of lymphoid follicles in the lymph nodes were observed at 1 mg/kg IV and SC. Findings in the liver included with hypertrophy/hyperplasia of sinusoidal Kupffer cells at \ge 0.1 mg/kg IV. These findings were noted during the recovery necropsies with lower incidence on Day 22 in the spleen, lymph nodes and liver and limited to 0.1 and 1 mg/kg (IV and SC), and on Day 57 in the spleen and lymph nodes at 1 mg/kg IV. In CNS tissues, perivascular (generally) infiltrates of eosinophils with associated microgliosis were observed in the brain and/or spinal cord and/or spinal nerve roots at 1 mg/kg IV and SC. These changes were not associated with clinical observations. No CNS findings were noted on Days 22 or 57.
L256: BTCT4465A induced pharmacodynamic effects of rapid and sustained B-cell depletion in blood and lymphoid tissues, increases in serum BAFF levels, dose-dependent reductions in T lymphocyte populations (total, T-helper, and T-cytotoxic) and NK cells, T-cell activation associated T-cell redistribution (total, Th and Tc lymphocytes), and cytokine/chemokine increases (IL-1RA, IL-2, IL-5, IL-6, IL-13, IL-17, G-CSF, TNF-\alpha, and IFN-\gamma) in all groups (\ge 0.01 mg/kg) at the 2 hours post-dose time point. These changes trended toward recovery by Day 8 through Day 57.
L257: ## 5.5.2. Genetic Toxicology
L258: ## The Applicant's Position:
L259: In accordance with the current ICH guidance (ICH S6(R1) 2011), genotoxicity studies have not been conducted with mosunetuzumab because the potential of biotechnology products, in particular high molecular weight monoclonal antibodies, to elicit DNA damage in somatic or germ cells via electrophilic reactions is unlikely.
L260: ## The FDA's Assessment:
L261: The FDA concurs with the Applicant.
L262: ## 5.5.3. Carcinogenicity
L263: ## The Applicant's Position:
L264: No carcinogenicity studies have been conducted with mosunetuzumab and are not planned for the intended oncology indication, as per Guidance for Industry S6R1.
L265: ## The FDA's Assessment:
L266: The FDA concurs with the Applicant.
L267: ## 5.5.4. Reproductive and Developmental Toxicology
L268: ## The Applicant's Position:
L269: Male and female reproductive assessments were conducted as a part of the 26-week chronic toxicity study in sexually mature cynomolgus monkeys (Study 16-1815). Evaluation included organ weights, and macroscopic and microscopic evaluation of male and female reproductive organs (including qualitative spermatogenesis progression for males and general phases of menstrual cycles for females). There were no mosunetuzumab-related effects identified in the male or female reproductive assessment.
L270: A weight of evidence (WOE)-based risk assessment for potential developmental effects was conducted. Based on low placental transfer of antibodies during the first trimester, the mechanism of action and available data of mosunetuzumab, and the data from other approved anti-CD20 antibodies developed by the Applicant (e.g., rituximab [Rituxan® USPI], obinutuzumab [Gazyva® USPI], ocrelizumab [Ocrevus® USPI]), the risk for teratogenicity is low. Studies with mosunetuzumab in non-pregnant animals have demonstrated that prolonged B-cell depletion in the dam can lead to increased risk of opportunistic infection, which may result in fetal loss, as shown with ocrelizumab [Ocrevus® USPI]). Transient CRS associated with mosunetuzumab administration may also be harmful to pregnancy.
L271: A briefing book, including the WOE-based risk assessment and a request to waive a non-human primate embryo-fetal and prenatal/postnatal development study, was submitted to the FDA (dated 3 April 2018; Reference ID: 4277993). In a written communication from the FDA dated 14 June 2018, the Agency agreed to the waiver request. During the End-of-Phase-I Type B meeting on 18 March, 2020, the Agency recommended to remove the list of products for which the Sponsor does not have the right-to-reference, and considered other components used in the WOE-based risk assessment reasonable for submission. The updated risk assessment document is included in Module 4 (Assessment of Developmental Effects).
L272: ## The FDA's Assessment:
L273: To address the potential effects of mosunetuzumab on embryo-fetal development, the Applicant submitted an assessment for reproductive and developmental risk using a weight-of-evidence (WOE) approach and with the recommendation in the ICH S6(R1) guideline. The relevant information is summarized below and supports that the main reproductive risk for mosunetuzumab is B-cell depletion in neonates and includes information from other products that the Applicant (Genentech, Inc.) owns or have the right of reference to.
L274: The WOE risk assessment includes the following information; references cited were provided by the Applicant:
L275: - Maternal and/or neonatal toxicities have been observed with the FDA approved anti-CD20 antibodies, including rituximab, obinutuzumab and ocrelizumab.Obinutuzumab crosses the blood-placental barrier and it is secreted in the milk of pregnant monkeys. There was complete depletion of B lymphocytes in infants, and higher prenatal loss in treatment groups compared to controls but within the range of historical control data.
- Based on its mechanism of action and pharmacodynamic effects seen in animals, mosunetuzumab may cause depletion of B-cells and impair the immune response after birth due to fetal exposure to mosunetuzumab. Impaired immune response may likely be associated with opportunistic infections. Secondary infections due to B-cell depletion and associated mortality/early euthanasia were observed in cynomolgus monkeys following prolonged treatment with mosunetuzumab in the 26-week toxicity study.
- Mosunetuzumab-related toxicities including vomiting, diarrhea, hypoactivity/hunched posture, hypotension, tachycardia, fever, acute phase protein reactions, leukocyte margination, in cynomolgus monkeys may pose a risk to the maintenance of pregnancy.
L276: ## 5.5.5. Other Toxicology Studies
L277: ## The Applicant's Position:
L278: An in vitro assay was performed in human PBMCs to assess cytokine release and T cell activation induced by mosunetuzumab. The results (EC_{20} values) were used to inform the starting dose in the first in human study. Additionally, the same assay was used to evaluate the potential impact of [REDACTED](b)(4) a product-related variant that may be formed at low levels in the production of mosunetuzumab, on the activities of mosunetuzumab. The results were used to support the proposed control strategy [REDACTED](b)(4) for the commercial material. A tissue cross-reactivity study could not be conducted due to poor analytical sensitivity and specificity of mosunetuzumab as an IHC reagent.
L279: ## The FDA's Assessment:
L280: BTCT4465A induces B-cell killing, T-cell activation, and cytokine production in human PBMCs from healthy donors in a concentration-dependent manner (Study: 14-3526). Low levels of (b) (4) spiked in BTCT4465A had minimal impact on BTCT4456A-induced, target cell-dependent T-cell activation and cytokine release in whole PBMCs, and did not appear to inhibit BTCT4456A-induced B-cell killing.
L281: The Agency concurs with the Applicant that BTCT4465A exhibited both poor analytical sensitivity and specificity as an IHC reagent and thus, tissue cross-reactivity in human and cynomolgus monkey tissue panels was not determined (Study: 14-2073).

L288: ## 6 Clinical Pharmacology
L289: ## 6.1. Executive Summary
L290: ## The FDA's Assessment:
L291: Mosunetuzumab is an anti-CD20/CD3 T-cell-dependent bispecific (TDB) antibody targeting both CD3 (on the surface of T cells) and CD20 (on the surface of B cells). The Applicant is seeking approval of mosunetuzumab for the treatment of patients with relapsed or refractory (R/R) follicular lymphoma (FL) who have received at least two prior systemic therapies. The proposed dosing regimen is shown in Table 9 below (hereafter referred to as 1/2/60/30 mg). The proposed treatment duration is 8 cycles initially; if patients received PR or SD, continue treatment for up to 1 year (17 cycles).
L292: **Table 9: Recommended Dose and Schedule (21-Day Treatment Cycles)**
L293: Day of Treatment	Dose
60
L294: *Version date: January 2020 (ALL NDA/ BLA reviews)*
L295: *Disclaimer: In this document, the sections labeled as "Data" and "The Applicant's Position" are completed by the Applicant and do not necessarily reflect the positions of the FDA.*
L296: ## BLA Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation {BLA 761263, Lunsumio (Mosunetuzumab)}
L297: Cycle 1	Day 1	1 mg
Day 8	2 mg
Day 15	60 mg
Cycle 2	Day 1	60 mg
Cycles 3+	Day 1	30 mg
L298: The primary efficacy and safety evidence supporting the proposed dosage regimen results from the pivotal B11 expansion cohort in Study GO29781, an ongoing first-in-human, multicenter, open-label, Phase I/II dose escalation and expansion study demonstrating an objective response rate (ORR) of 80% and a complete response rate (CR) of 60%, as well as an overall acceptable safety profile.
L299: The key review issues focused on the evaluation of dose selection, evaluation of dosage recommendations for restarting therapy after dose delay, and drug-drug interaction (DDI) due to cytokine release.
L300: ## *Recommendations*
L301: The Office of Clinical Pharmacology has reviewed the information submitted in BLA761263. This BLA is approvable from a clinical pharmacology perspective. The key review issues with specific recommendations/comments are summarized below in Table 10.
L302: **Table 10: Key Clinical Pharmacology Review Issues and Recommendations by FDA**
L303: Review Issue	Recommendations and Comments
Pivotal and Supportive evidence of effectiveness	The primary evidence of effectiveness comes from the pivotal B11 FL RP2D cohort in study GO29781. The proposed dosage regimen (1/2/60/30 mg) is supported by an ORR of 80% and CR of 60% in patients with relapsed or refractory FL.
General dosing instructions	The proposed dosage regimen (1/2/60/30 mg) is selected with the following rationale:The step-up doses (1 mg and 2 mg) are clinically active doses that help mitigate CRS risk in later cycles.
No exposure response trends were observed for safety endpoints except for Grade \ge 2 CRS, at the proposed dose of 1/2/60/30 mg, the observed Grade \ge 2 CRS and the Exposure-Response model predicted that the probability for Grade \ge 2 CRS over the first two cycles is relatively low.
There were positive exposure response relationships for
L304: efficacy endpoints, such as CR, ORR and PFS.
Tumor size continued to decrease after shifting from loading dose (60 mg) to base dose (30 mg).
The treatment duration is supported by observed efficacy data showing that:
Majority of CR (50 out of 54 cases) were achieved within 8 cycles of treatment.
Patients who received PR or SD after 8 cycles of treatment benefited (deepened response from PR to CR, or received late PR/CR) from continued treatment beyond 8 cycles.

Dosage recommendations for restarting therapy after dose delay	
The proposed dosage recommendation for restarting therapy after dose delay is to mitigate CRS risk and is supported by:
Observed clinical data showing that CRS was mitigated.
PopPK simulations showing that mosunetuzumab concentrations were maintained approximately at or above concentration levels achieved with 2 mg prior to administration of the 60 mg loading dose, consistent with the proposed dosing regimen (1/2/60/30 mg) and the associated CRS mitigation strategy.

Dosing in patient subgroups (intrinsic and extrinsic factors)	
No dosage adjustment is needed in patient subgroups. The effect of severe renal impairment to end-stage renal disease (CL_{Cr} < 30 mL/min) and moderate to severe hepatic impairment (total bilirubin > 3 to 10x ULN, any AST) is unknown.

Drug-drug interactions	
Mosunetuzumab administration resulted in transient release of cytokines, which may suppress CYP450 enzymes and cause drug-drug interactions. Increased exposure of CYP450 substrates is more likely to occur after the first dose of mosunetuzumab on Cycle 1 Day 1 and up to 14 days after the second loading dose on Cycle 2 Day 1 and during and after CRS. Monitor for toxicity or concentrations of drugs that are CYP450 substrates where minimal concentration changes may lead to serious adverse reactions. Adjust the dose of the concomitant drug as needed.

Labeling	
Overall, the proposed labeling recommendations are acceptable upon the Applicant's agreement with the FDA recommended revisions to the labeling.
L305: ## 6.2. Summary of Clinical Pharmacology Assessment
L306: ## 6.2.1. Pharmacology and Clinical Pharmacokinetics
L307: ## **Data:**
L308: The evaluation of the clinical pharmacology of mosunetuzumab to support this BLA application was based on clinical data from the IV monotherapy cohorts of Study GO29781, including the fixed dosing group (Group A; 0.05-2.8 mg IV q3w) and the Cycle 1 step-up dosing group (Group B; 0.4/1/2.8-1/2/60 mg IV q3w) in patients with R/R NHL.
L309: - Characterization of mosunetuzumab clinical serum PK, including assessment of distribution and elimination characteristics, dose linearity characterization, source of PK variability, and individual-level covariates for mosunetuzumab in patients with NHL, are provided.
- The integrated E-R analyses, combined with the observed clinical efficacy and safety data, provide strong support for the RP2D/intended registration dose and schedule for mosunetuzumab in patients with R/R FL.
L310: See Section 6.3.1 for details.
L311: ## **The Applicant's Position:**
L312: Collectively, the PK and E-R analyses support the intended registration dose and schedule for mosunetuzumab in patients with R/R FL and support a positive benefit-risk profile.
L313: ## **The FDA's Assessment:**
L314: The mosunetuzumab clinical pharmacology program included data from two groups (Group A and Group B) in study GO29781 (Table 11, Table 12).

L319: aDosing regimen in B11 cohort is the intended registration dose and schedule, 1 mg on C1D1, 2 mg on C1D8, 60 mg on C1D15 and C2D1, 30 mg on D1 of subsequent cycles.
L320: bB11 expansion phase included R/R FL patients (N=90), R/R DLBCL/trFL patients (N=88), R/R MCL patients (N=25), R/R Richter's patients (N=14) and other (N=1).
L321: The results from these groups and cohorts generally provide adequate characterization of mosunetuzumab PK properties.
L322: **Absorption:** The drug product is administered intravenously; no food effect is expected.
L323: **Distribution:** The mean (CV%) volume of distribution of mosunetuzumab-axgb is 5.49 L (31%).
L324: **Elimination:** The steady-state mean (CV%) terminal elimination half-life of mosunetuzumab-axgb is 16.1 (17.3%) days. The geometric mean (CV%) clearance at baseline and at steady state are 1.08 L/day (63%) and 0.584 L/day (18%), respectively.
L325: **Dose proportionality:** Mosunetuzumab exposures increase in an approximately dose-proportional manner in the dose range studied (0.2–60 mg).
L326: ## 6.2.2. General Dosing and Therapeutic Individualization
L327: ## 6.2.2.1. General Dosing
L328: Data:
L329: The intended registration dose and schedule of mosunetuzumab is administered using the Cycle 1 step-up regimen (i.e., 1 mg and 2 mg on Cycle 1 Day 1 and 8, respectively, as the “step doses”, followed by two “loading doses” of 60 mg on Cycle 1 Day 15 and Cycle 2 Day 1, and then maintained at the “base dose” of 30 mg for the remaining Cycles 3–8; treatment continues for 17 cycles [approximately 1 year] if CR is not achieved by Cycle 8), hereafter referred to as 1/2/60/30 mg.
L330: A schematic of the intended registration dose and schedule is shown in Figure 9.
L331: APPEARS THIS WAY ON ORIGINAL
L332: **Figure 9: Applicant – Conceptual Illustration of the Intended Registration Dose and Schedule* for Mosunetuzumab in R/R FL**
L333: *With the option to dose up to 17 cycles if complete response is not achieved by Cycle 8.
L334: The rationale for the intended registration dose and schedule of 1/2/60/30 mg is summarized below:
L335: - “Step Doses” of 1 mg and 2 mg on Cycle 1 Day 1 and 8 are pharmacodynamically and clinically active and well tolerated doses shown to mitigate the maximum receptor occupancy (RO_{max})-dependency of the acute CRS.
- “Loading Doses” of 60 mg on Cycle 1 Day 15 and Cycle 2 Day 1 are predicted to achieve maximal clinical response (i.e., CRR and ORR) by exposure-response analyses in the proposed patient population.
- “Base Doses” of 30 mg on Day 1 in each cycle in Cycles 3 and beyond are expected to achieve maximal and durable clinical response following the initial tumor debulking after the first 2 cycles based on the totality of data.
L336: The model-predictions based on integrated ER analyses of efficacy and safety for Study GO29781 including patients with R/R FL indicate a broad therapeutic window of mosunetuzumab with minimal/low exposure-dependency for the overall safety risk using the Cycle 1 step-up dosing regimen. The integrated ER analyses, combined with the observed clinical efficacy and safety data, provide strong support for the RP2D/intended registration dose and schedule of 1/2/60/30 mg to achieve a favorable clinical benefit-risk profile in patients with R/R FL, with high clinical response rate (model-predicted probability for CRR and ORR of approximately 63% and 79%, respectively) and low (&lt;12%) probability of a patient experiencing
L337: Grade \ge 2 CRS that is manageable and primarily confined to Cycle 1 (Figure 10). Overall occurrence of Grade \ge 3 CRS observed at the clinical dose was low (&lt;3%) in patients in the B11 RP2D cohort. The RP2D/intended registration dose and schedule of 1/2/60/30 mg is expected to achieve PK exposures that maximize clinical efficacy for the majority of the patient population with manageable safety profiles.
L338: **Figure 10: Applicant – Integrated Understanding of Mosunetuzumab Clinical Dose-Response Relationships Predicted by Modeling for CR, OR and Grade \ge 2 CRS**
L339: CR= complete response; CRS= cytokine release syndrome; Inv= investigator; OR= objective response.
L340: Note: base models (i.e., without covariates) used for prediction.
L341: Filled circles represent the model-predicted probability of response (blue for CR, green for OR, red for of Grade \ge 2 CRS) at selective dose levels; Points connected by solid lines are medians; dashed lines connect geometric means; shaded areas represent 50%, 90%, and 95% confidence intervals.
L342: For the 1/2/60/30 mg dose regimen, the modelling predicts 1) a median CRR of 63.1% (95% prediction interval = 49.7–75.0%), 2) a median ORR of 79.1% (69.1–87.7%), and 3) a median Grade \ge 2 CRS rate of 11.8% (5.27–23.2%). For the 1/2/30 mg dose regimen, the modelling predicts 1) a median CRR of 58.3% (95% prediction interval = 39.90–68.8%), 2) a median ORR of 76.4% (60.9–84.4%), and 3) a median Grade \ge 2 CRS rate of 9.75% (4.90–16.3%). For the 1/2/13.5 mg dose regimen, the modelling predicts 1) a median CRR of 50.2% (95% prediction interval = 27.7–62.0%), 2) a median ORR of 71.0% (43.9–79.8%), and 3) a median Grade \ge 2 CRS rate of 8.27% (4.60–12.3%).
L343: ## The Applicant's Position:
L344: The totality of data including the pharmacological expectation, the integrated E-R analyses for efficacy and safety, and the observed clinical efficacy and safety, consistently support the rationale for selection of the 1/2/60/30 mg as the intended registration dose and schedule of mosunetuzumab in patients with R/R FL \ge2 prior therapies.
L345: ## The FDA's Assessment:
L346: FDA agrees with the Applicant's position that the proposed dosing regimen of mosunetuzumab for the general patient population (1/2/60/30 mg) is acceptable. The dose selection is based on the following rationale:
L347: - Priming dosing regimenThe priming doses 1 mg and 2 mg are clinically active doses. In patients with FL who received \ge 2 prior therapies, the minimum efficacious dose is 1.2 mg, as shown in Table 13.

L373: Of note, exploratory covariates analysis showed that histology and age are significant covariates of occurrence of Grade \ge 2 CRS; patients with histologies other than DLBCL, transformed follicular lymphoma (trFL), or FL, and patients \le 64 years old, are predicted to have a higher probability of Grade &gt; 2 CRS. See details in Section 20.4.5.
L374: ## - **Loading doses and base doses**
L375: - Positive Exposure-Response relationships were observed for efficacy endpoints, such as CR, OR, and PFS.
- No Exposure-Response Trends were observed for safety endpoints, higher mosunetuzumab exposure up to the proposed dose of 1/2/60/30 mg did not lead to earlier onset time or higher incidence of any Grade \ge 1 AEs, any Grade \ge 3 AEs, Grade \ge 3 Neutropenia, Grade \ge 3 Infections and infestations or any Grade Neurological AEs.
- Tumor size continued to decrease after shifting from loading dose to base dose. See details on Exposure-Response relationships in section 20.4.5.
L376: ## - **Treatment duration**
L377: - Majority of CRs (50 out of 54 cases) were achieved within 8 cycles of treatment based on the IRF assessment. Median duration of CR has not been reached.
- Only 16 out of 90 efficacy evaluable patients received more than 8 cycles of treatment.
L378: Based on the IRF assessment, 5 patients had a late initial response or deepening of a response with additional treatment cycles. These included: 3 patients who had a deepening of response from PR to CR, 1 patient who had an initial PR after 13 cycles of treatment and 1 patient who had an initial CR after 13 cycles of treatment. Despite the small sample size, available clinical data provided supportive evidence that patients who receive PR or SD after 8 cycles of treatment may benefit from continued treatment beyond 8 cycles.
L379: ## 6.2.2.2. Therapeutic Individualization
L380: ## **Data:**
L381: The mosunetuzumab population PK (PopPK) analysis dataset included data from 439 PK-evaluable patients from Study GO29781 who received mosunetuzumab by IV administration. Body weight, tumor burden, albumin, previous anti-CD20 drug concentrations, and sex were found to be statistically significant covariates on mosunetuzumab PK with marginal impact on drug exposure. None of these covariates resulted in more than 20% change in mosunetuzumab exposure (area under the concentration-time curve from time 0 to 42 days, AUC_{0-42}), when evaluated at extreme values compared to the typical patients. Neither patient age, renal impairment status, hepatic impairment status, drug material used, histology (i.e., FL vs. DLBCL vs. other), nor race (Asian vs. non-Asian) were identified as covariates influencing mosunetuzumab PK. Effects of covariates of special interest (body weight, age, gender, race, hepatic function and renal function, NHL histology) on mosunetuzumab PK were summarized below:
L382: ## **Body Weight**
L383: The body weight effect on mosunetuzumab exposure was relatively small compared to the overall inter-individual variability of the population and was not clinically relevant. There does not appear to be an impact of body size on efficacy of mosunetuzumab.
L384: ## **Age**
L385: Age had no statistically significant impact on the mosunetuzumab PK parameters. The age effect on mosunetuzumab exposure was relatively small compared to the overall inter-individual variability of the population and was not clinically relevant. There were no differences in safety and efficacy of mosunetuzumab observed by age subgroup.
L386: ## **Gender**
L387: The mosunetuzumab AUC_{0-42} for a typical female patient (weighing 78 kg) is approximately 6% higher than a typical male patient and not considered clinically meaningful. There were no differences in safety and efficacy of mosunetuzumab observed by gender subgroup.
L388: ## **Race**
L389: Race (Asian vs. non-Asian) had no statistically significant impact on the mosunetuzumab PK
L390: parameters. The race effect on mosunetuzumab exposure was relatively small compared to the overall inter-individual variability of the population and was not clinically relevant. There were no differences in efficacy of mosunetuzumab observed by race subgroup.
L391: ## Hepatic Impairment
L392: None of the lab values tested (i.e., AST, ALT, total bilirubin) was a statistically significant covariate on mosunetuzumab PK. Hepatic function category had no apparent effect on model predicted mosunetuzumab PK exposure. The limited data indicated that mosunetuzumab exposures in patients with mild hepatic impairment were not meaningfully different compared to those with normal hepatic function. The number of patients with moderate hepatic impairment is limited (n=2) and no patients with severe hepatic impairment have been studied. No dose recommendation can be made for mosunetuzumab in patients with moderate or severe hepatic impairment.
L393: ## Renal Impairment
L394: CrCL was not a statistically significant covariate on mosunetuzumab PK. Renal function category had no apparent effect on model predicted mosunetuzumab PK exposure. However, the number of patients with severe renal impairment is limited (n=1) and no patients on dialysis have been studied, and a recommended dose for mosunetuzumab has not been determined for these patients.
L395: ## NHL histology
L396: NHL histology (DLBCL, FL, and others) had no statistically significant impact on the mosunetuzumab PK parameters. Mosunetuzumab PK exposure (AUC_{0-42}) is comparable across NHL histology groups.
L397: ## The Applicant's Position:
L398: Given the totality of the data based on results from the mosunetuzumab PopPK analysis, together with subgroup analysis for the efficacy and safety, no clinically meaningful covariate was identified that warrants dose adjustment for mosunetuzumab.
L399: ## The FDA's Assessment:
L400: The FDA agrees with the Applicant's position. The Applicant's population PK analysis for mosunetuzumab is considered acceptable for the purpose of supporting analyses objectives. Body weight (37.1 to 163 kg), tumor burden (96.0 to 70900 mm^2), albumin (19.0 to 480 g/L), baseline residual anti-CD20 drug concentrations, and sex (Female [n = 155, 35.3%] and Male [n = 284, 64.7%]) were found to be statistically significant covariates on mosunetuzumab pharmacokinetics with marginal impact on drug exposure that is not clinically meaningful (Figure 15).
L401: Figure 15: Forest Plots of Covariates Effects on AUC0-42
L402: In addition, due to the limited sample size in races other than White and Asian, the Applicant only evaluated the PK difference in Asian vs. non-Asian. A further breakdown in races was shown in Figure 16. However, a comparison of the PK differences among race groups is limited by the sample size. The FDA will issue a PMR to conduct a randomized clinical trial in patients with relapsed or refractory follicular lymphoma to verify the clinical benefit of mosunetuzumab. The trial should include sufficient representation of racial and ethnic minorities to adequately reflect the U.S. patient population and allow for interpretation of the results in these patient populations. The PopPK analysis will be updated when more information becomes available to evaluate the PK differences among race groups.
L403: Figure 16: Comparison of AUC0-42 by Race at 1/2/60/30 mg

L409: The proposed dosage recommendations are based on the same instructions included in the GO29781 Study Protocol and the rationale to step-up dosing following the above-described dose delay scenarios is to mitigate the risk of CRS. While CRS associated with mosunetuzumab treatment occurs predominantly during the first cycle of treatment, the risk may re-emerge
L410: when a patient is off treatment for a prolonged period of time. Therefore, to mitigate an increase of CRS risk in patients with dose delay, a repeat step-up dosing is recommended. In addition, in all scenarios outlined above, the recommendation ensures that the patient receives both 60 mg loading doses followed by the continued 30 mg Q3W doses in subsequent cycles to maintain cumulative on-study exposure similar to that achieved with the nominal proposed dosing regimen of 1/2/60/30 mg, given the established positive benefit-risk profile at this dosage and the positive E-R relationship for efficacy.
L411: The above recommendations are supported by:
L412: - Observed clinical data showing that among the 14 patients who met the above dose delay scenarios, 13 out of 14 followed the recommendations and did not experience CRS after treatment resumption. One patient experienced CRS after treatment resumption, however, this patient was enrolled before the proposed recommendations became effective following a protocol amendment and did not follow the above recommendations.
- Supportive population PK simulations showed that when the dose delay was longer, mosunetuzumab concentration would fall below the drug levels obtained with the step up doses (1 mg and 2 mg) on Cycle 1 Day 1 and Day 8, putting the patient at increased risk of CRS due to potential resensitization to mosunetuzumab treatment; repeat step-up doses as recommended above will maintain mosunetuzumab concentrations at or above concentration levels achieved with 2 mg prior to administration of the higher 60 mg loading dose and 30 mg base dose, consistent with the proposed dosing regimen (1/2/60/30 mg) and the associated CRS mitigation strategy. Population PK Model Simulations showing different dose delay scenarios are shown in Figure 17, Figure 18, Figure 19, Figure 20, Figure 21 and Figure 22 below.
L413: Figure 17: Nominal Intended Registration Dose and Regimen
